Well, I'm here because the clinic for my logistics job wouldn't clear me. They said my weight was too low, which is just ridiculous if you ask me. I mean, to me, I just look lean, you know? Like someone who's ready to work, stay quick on his feet. You have to be quick in freight, otherwise you're just in the way. I remember this one guy, back in '98, big guy, thought he could muscle everything. But he was so slow, tripped over a pallet of dog food and nearly took out the whole racking system. It took us three hours to clean up that mess, and the smell of that dog food... man, it just lingered for days. I can still smell it sometimes when the humidity gets high. But yeah, they said I'm too light to work, which makes no sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I mean, my sister and that clinic doctor keep saying I’m in some kind of danger. But they're just making it way bigger than it is. They don’t understand that I’m just keeping myself light, sharp, and ready for work. You gotta be sharp on the floor. 

Just last week, this new kid—barely twenty, I swear—was driving the forklift like an absolute maniac. Came whipping right around the blind corner near the loading docks. If I wasn't light on my feet, he would've pinned me right against the corrugated steel wall. And let me tell you, those steel panels aren't cheap to replace. We had a driver back in 2012 back a whole rig into one of the bay doors, and it took management three months to get the contractor out to fix it. The draft that came through that hole all winter was brutal. We had to wear double thermals just to feel our fingers. My sister was always trying to knit me these thick wool socks back then, but wool just makes my feet sweat in the work boots, and once your boots are wet, you're miserable all shift. 

But yeah, they're just blowing the whole weight thing out of proportion. I'm completely fine.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not like I'm terrified of it, but... I mean, yeah, I guess I do worry about it. If I eat rice, bread, or something greasy, I can’t stop thinking it will just sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or my belt the next morning to make sure I didn’t let myself go. You know how it is, you let your guard down for one second and suddenly everything's out of control. 

Kind of like when they switched our inventory system at the warehouse back in '15. We used to do everything on paper, clipboards and carbon copies. It was a good system, you always had a hard copy if the computers went down. But then corporate brought in these new scanners, said it would make us "more efficient." Half the guys didn't even know how to turn the things on. I remember Jimmy—he was a good guy, retired a few years back, moved down to Florida. He hated the humidity down there, said it ruined his golf swing. But anyway, Jimmy accidentally scanned the same pallet of engine blocks like five times, and the system thought we had enough inventory to supply a whole assembly line. The trucking company sent three extra rigs the next day just to pick up blocks we didn't even have. The logistics manager was screaming so loud his face turned purple. 

But yeah, I just... I check my belt, make sure I'm still tight, still ready to move. You can't afford to get sluggish.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... yeah, I keep things pretty tight. Most days I just stick to black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little bit of tuna. If I'm not actually on the floor moving freight, I just tell myself I don't really need a real meal anyway. You don't need the fuel if the engine ain't running, right? 

Speaking of engines, my buddy Carl—he works over at the auto parts distribution center across town—he was telling me about this whole shipment of alternators they got in last month. Apparently, they were all defective straight from the manufacturer. Just totally shot. Carl said they had to load 'em all back onto the pallets, wrap 'em up, and ship 'em right back to Mexico. The shrink wrap they use over there is garbage, though. It's that thin, cheap stuff that tears if you even look at it funny. I always tell our purchasing guy to buy the heavy-duty wrap, the 80-gauge stuff. It costs a few cents more a roll, but it saves you hours of re-wrapping when a pallet shifts in transit. I remember one time we had a whole skid of windshield washer fluid tip over inside a trailer because some rookie used the cheap wrap. Blue liquid everywhere. Smelled like windex for a month. 

But yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Just keep it light so I don't get weighed down.

Came whipping right around the blind corner near the loading docks. If I wasn't light on my feet, he would've pinned me right against the corrugated steel wall. And let me tell you, those steel panels aren't cheap to replace. We had a driver back in 2012 back a whole rig into one of the bay doors, and it took management three months to get the contractor out to fix it. The draft that came through that hole all winter was brutal. We had to wear double thermals just to feel our fingers. My sister was always trying to knit me these thick wool socks back then, but wool just makes my feet sweat in the work boots, and once your boots are wet, you're miserable all shift. \n\nBut yeah, they're just blowing the whole weight thing out of proportion. I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I'm terrified of it, but... I mean, yeah, I guess I do worry about it. If I eat rice, bread, or something greasy, I can’t stop thinking it will just sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or my belt the next morning to make sure I didn’t let myself go. You know how it is, you let your guard down for one second and suddenly everything's out of control. \n\nKind of like when they switched our inventory system at the warehouse back in '15. We used to do everything on paper, clipboards and carbon copies. It was a good system, you always had a hard copy if the computers went down. But then corporate brought in these new scanners, said it would make us \"more efficient.\" Half the guys didn't even know how to turn the things on. I remember Jimmy—he was a good guy, retired a few years back, moved down to Florida. He hated the humidity down there, said it ruined his golf swing. But anyway, Jimmy accidentally scanned the same pallet of engine blocks like five times, and the system thought we had enough inventory to supply a whole assembly line. The trucking company sent three extra rigs the next day just to pick up blocks we didn't even have. The logistics manager was screaming so loud his face turned purple. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I check my belt, make sure I'm still tight, still ready to move. You can't afford to get sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, I keep things pretty tight. Most days I just stick to black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little bit of tuna. If I'm not actually on the floor moving freight, I just tell myself I don't really need a real meal anyway. You don't need the fuel if the engine ain't running, right? \n\nSpeaking of engines, my buddy Carl—he works over at the auto parts distribution center across town—he was telling me about this whole shipment of alternators they got in last month. Apparently, they were all defective straight from the manufacturer. Just totally shot. Carl said they had to load 'em all back onto the pallets, wrap 'em up, and ship 'em right back to Mexico. The shrink wrap they use over there is garbage, though. It's that thin, cheap stuff that tears if you even look at it funny. I always tell our purchasing guy to buy the heavy-duty wrap, the 80-gauge stuff. It costs a few cents more a roll, but it saves you hours of re-wrapping when a pallet shifts in transit. I remember one time we had a whole skid of windshield washer fluid tip over inside a trailer because some rookie used the cheap wrap. Blue liquid everywhere. Smelled like windex for a month. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Just keep it light so I don't get weighed down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would look good if you gained a little weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / laborer_or_freight_stock_or_material_mover / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like if I’m not smaller and tight-looking then I’m not the disciplined man I used to be on the warehouse floor.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Look good? No, no way. If I gained weight... I mean, when I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, then I’m just... I'm not the disciplined man I used to be on the warehouse floor. You lose that edge, you know? 

It’s like those old conveyor belts we had in the sorting facility before the remodel. They were these heavy, thick rubber belts, and over time, if they got even a little slack in them, they’d start catching on the rollers. Once a belt catches, it throws the whole line off. I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. We had guys standing around doing nothing for 48 hours while the freight just piled up on the dock. The regional manager came down, and he was livid, just screaming about metrics and throughput. But you can't argue with a broken motor, right? 

But yeah, if I get soft around the middle, I just... I don't look right. I have to stay tight.

I check my belt, make sure I'm still tight, still ready to move. Most days I just stick to black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little bit of tuna. If I'm not actually on the floor moving freight, I just tell myself I don't really need a real meal anyway. You don't need the fuel if the engine ain't running, right? \n\nSpeaking of engines, my buddy Carl—he works over at the auto parts distribution center across town—he was telling me about this whole shipment of alternators they got in last month. Apparently, they were all defective straight from the manufacturer. Just totally shot. Carl said they had to load 'em all back onto the pallets, wrap 'em up, and ship 'em right back to Mexico. The shrink wrap they use over there is garbage, though. It's that thin, cheap stuff that tears if you even look at it funny. I always tell our purchasing guy to buy the heavy-duty wrap, the 80-gauge stuff. It costs a few cents more a roll, but it saves you hours of re-wrapping when a pallet shifts in transit. I remember one time we had a whole skid of windshield washer fluid tip over inside a trailer because some rookie used the cheap wrap. Blue liquid everywhere. Smelled like windex for a month. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Just keep it light so I don't get weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a little weight?\n\nPatient: Look good? No, no way. If I gained weight... I mean, when I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, then I’m just... I'm not the disciplined man I used to be on the warehouse floor. You lose that edge, you know? \n\nIt’s like those old conveyor belts we had in the sorting facility before the remodel. They were these heavy, thick rubber belts, and over time, if they got even a little slack in them, they’d start catching on the rollers. Once a belt catches, it throws the whole line off. I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. We had guys standing around doing nothing for 48 hours while the freight just piled up on the dock. The regional manager came down, and he was livid, just screaming about metrics and throughput. But you can't argue with a broken motor, right? \n\nBut yeah, if I get soft around the middle, I just... I don't look right. I have to stay tight.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you look too thin now?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / laborer_or_freight_stock_or_material_mover / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\nmanifestation: \"My sister and the doctor keep saying I’m in danger, but I think they’re making it bigger than it is because they don’t understand I’m just keeping myself light, sharp, and ready for work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Too thin? I mean, my sister keeps harping on it, and my regular doctor acts like I'm about to drop dead or something. They keep saying I'm in some kind of danger, but they're making a mountain out of a molehill. They just don't get it. I'm just keeping myself light, you know? Sharp. Ready for work. If you're hauling freight, you can't be dragging extra weight around. 

Speaking of hauling freight, we had this new guy start last week, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Showed up on his first day wearing these pristine, bright white sneakers. I took one look at him and told him those shoes wouldn't last till lunch. Sure enough, by ten o'clock he dropped a pallet jack right on his toe. Didn't break it, but the grease stain on that white mesh... ruined 'em. I always tell these kids, you gotta get steel-toe boots. I bought my Red Wings back in 2018, and they're still holding up. I get them resoled every couple of years by this cobbler downtown. Real old-school guy, his shop smells like leather polish and cigar smoke. He uses this heavy-duty stitching that you just don't see anymore. Most boots you buy off the shelf now, the soles start peeling off after six months because they just glue them on. It's cheap manufacturing, is what it is. 

But yeah, my sister and the doctor... they just don't understand that I'm just staying sharp for the floor. I'm not in any danger.

I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. We had guys standing around doing nothing for 48 hours while the freight just piled up on the dock. The regional manager came down, and he was livid, just screaming about metrics and throughput. But you can't argue with a broken motor, right? \n\nBut yeah, if I get soft around the middle, I just... I don't look right. I have to stay tight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I mean, my sister keeps harping on it, and my regular doctor acts like I'm about to drop dead or something. They keep saying I'm in some kind of danger, but they're making a mountain out of a molehill. They just don't get it. I'm just keeping myself light, you know? Sharp. Ready for work. If you're hauling freight, you can't be dragging extra weight around. \n\nSpeaking of hauling freight, we had this new guy start last week, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Showed up on his first day wearing these pristine, bright white sneakers. I took one look at him and told him those shoes wouldn't last till lunch. Sure enough, by ten o'clock he dropped a pallet jack right on his toe. Didn't break it, but the grease stain on that white mesh... ruined 'em. I always tell these kids, you gotta get steel-toe boots. I bought my Red Wings back in 2018, and they're still holding up. I get them resoled every couple of years by this cobbler downtown. Real old-school guy, his shop smells like leather polish and cigar smoke. He uses this heavy-duty stitching that you just don't see anymore. Most boots you buy off the shelf now, the soles start peeling off after six months because they just glue them on. It's cheap manufacturing, is what it is. \n\nBut yeah, my sister and the doctor... they just don't understand that I'm just staying sharp for the floor. I'm not in any danger.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight is the same as other men your age and height?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Same as other guys? I don't know, I haven't really... I mean, I don't go around comparing myself to the other guys on the dock. Most of them are carrying a lot of extra baggage, if you ask me. 

Take Big Mike, for instance. We call him Big Mike because, well, the guy's built like a forklift. He must eat three meatball subs a day from that deli down on 4th Street. You know the one? They wrap the subs in that thick butcher paper, but the grease still soaks right through. Mike leaves those greasy wrappers all over the breakroom tables. It drives the janitor, Luis, absolutely crazy. Luis is this quiet guy, always listening to Spanish talk radio on his little portable speaker while he mops. He uses this industrial floor cleaner that smells like industrial pine and bleach. It's strong stuff. Actually, last winter, the heating system in the warehouse went out, and Luis accidentally left a bucket of that cleaner right by the loading bay doors. The whole bucket froze solid. We had to use a crowbar to chip it out so we could move the pallets. Took us an hour just to clear the ice. 

But yeah, I don't really pay attention to what I weigh compared to guys like Mike or anyone else. I'm just focused on my own thing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... afraid? It's just... if I eat a normal amount, like if I have rice or bread, or heaven forbid something greasy, I just... I can't stop thinking about it. I feel like it's just going to sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or pulling at my belt the next morning just to make sure I didn't let myself go. 

Speaking of belts, I've had the same leather work belt for fifteen years. It's got this heavy brass buckle that I bought at a flea market out by the old fairgrounds. You remember when they used to host that massive swap meet every summer? People would come from three states over just to sell junk out of their trunks. I bought that buckle from this guy selling vintage motorcycle parts. He had a whole table of carburetors and spark plugs, just covered in this fine layer of rust and dust. His dog, this massive Rottweiler mix, was just sleeping under the folding table the whole time. It was so hot that day, I think it was August of '09, the asphalt was literally soft under my boots. You could smell the funnel cakes from the food trucks mixing with the smell of exhaust... 

But yeah, if I eat that stuff, I just have to check the belt the next day to make sure I'm still tight. I can't afford to get soft.

I mean, to me, I just look lean, you know? Like someone who's ready to work, stay quick on his feet. You have to be quick in freight, otherwise you're just in the way. I remember this one guy, back in '98, big guy, thought he could muscle everything. But he was so slow, tripped over a pallet of dog food and nearly took out the whole racking system. It took us three hours to clean up that mess, and the smell of that dog food... man, it just lingered for days. I can still smell it sometimes when the humidity gets high. But yeah, they said I'm too light to work, which makes no sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, my sister and that clinic doctor keep saying I’m in some kind of danger. But they're just making it way bigger than it is. They don’t understand that I’m just keeping myself light, sharp, and ready for work. You gotta be sharp on the floor. \n\nJust last week, this new kid—barely twenty, I swear—was driving the forklift like an absolute maniac. Came whipping right around the blind corner near the loading docks. If I wasn't light on my feet, he would've pinned me right against the corrugated steel wall. And let me tell you, those steel panels aren't cheap to replace. We had a driver back in 2012 back a whole rig into one of the bay doors, and it took management three months to get the contractor out to fix it. The draft that came through that hole all winter was brutal. We had to wear double thermals just to feel our fingers. My sister was always trying to knit me these thick wool socks back then, but wool just makes my feet sweat in the work boots, and once your boots are wet, you're miserable all shift. \n\nBut yeah, they're just blowing the whole weight thing out of proportion. I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I'm terrified of it, but... I mean, yeah, I guess I do worry about it. If I eat rice, bread, or something greasy, I can’t stop thinking it will just sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or my belt the next morning to make sure I didn’t let myself go. You know how it is, you let your guard down for one second and suddenly everything's out of control. \n\nKind of like when they switched our inventory system at the warehouse back in '15. We used to do everything on paper, clipboards and carbon copies. It was a good system, you always had a hard copy if the computers went down. But then corporate brought in these new scanners, said it would make us \"more efficient.\" Half the guys didn't even know how to turn the things on. I remember Jimmy—he was a good guy, retired a few years back, moved down to Florida. He hated the humidity down there, said it ruined his golf swing. But anyway, Jimmy accidentally scanned the same pallet of engine blocks like five times, and the system thought we had enough inventory to supply a whole assembly line. The trucking company sent three extra rigs the next day just to pick up blocks we didn't even have. The logistics manager was screaming so loud his face turned purple. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I check my belt, make sure I'm still tight, still ready to move. You can't afford to get sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, I keep things pretty tight. Most days I just stick to black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little bit of tuna. If I'm not actually on the floor moving freight, I just tell myself I don't really need a real meal anyway. You don't need the fuel if the engine ain't running, right? \n\nSpeaking of engines, my buddy Carl—he works over at the auto parts distribution center across town—he was telling me about this whole shipment of alternators they got in last month. Apparently, they were all defective straight from the manufacturer. Just totally shot. Carl said they had to load 'em all back onto the pallets, wrap 'em up, and ship 'em right back to Mexico. The shrink wrap they use over there is garbage, though. It's that thin, cheap stuff that tears if you even look at it funny. I always tell our purchasing guy to buy the heavy-duty wrap, the 80-gauge stuff. It costs a few cents more a roll, but it saves you hours of re-wrapping when a pallet shifts in transit. I remember one time we had a whole skid of windshield washer fluid tip over inside a trailer because some rookie used the cheap wrap. Blue liquid everywhere. Smelled like windex for a month. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Just keep it light so I don't get weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a little weight?\n\nPatient: Look good? No, no way. If I gained weight... I mean, when I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, then I’m just... I'm not the disciplined man I used to be on the warehouse floor. You lose that edge, you know? \n\nIt’s like those old conveyor belts we had in the sorting facility before the remodel. They were these heavy, thick rubber belts, and over time, if they got even a little slack in them, they’d start catching on the rollers. Once a belt catches, it throws the whole line off. I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. We had guys standing around doing nothing for 48 hours while the freight just piled up on the dock. The regional manager came down, and he was livid, just screaming about metrics and throughput. But you can't argue with a broken motor, right? \n\nBut yeah, if I get soft around the middle, I just... I don't look right. I have to stay tight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I mean, my sister keeps harping on it, and my regular doctor acts like I'm about to drop dead or something. They keep saying I'm in some kind of danger, but they're making a mountain out of a molehill. They just don't get it. I'm just keeping myself light, you know? Sharp. Ready for work. If you're hauling freight, you can't be dragging extra weight around. \n\nSpeaking of hauling freight, we had this new guy start last week, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Showed up on his first day wearing these pristine, bright white sneakers. I took one look at him and told him those shoes wouldn't last till lunch. Sure enough, by ten o'clock he dropped a pallet jack right on his toe. Didn't break it, but the grease stain on that white mesh... ruined 'em. I always tell these kids, you gotta get steel-toe boots. I bought my Red Wings back in 2018, and they're still holding up. I get them resoled every couple of years by this cobbler downtown. Real old-school guy, his shop smells like leather polish and cigar smoke. He uses this heavy-duty stitching that you just don't see anymore. Most boots you buy off the shelf now, the soles start peeling off after six months because they just glue them on. It's cheap manufacturing, is what it is. \n\nBut yeah, my sister and the doctor... they just don't understand that I'm just staying sharp for the floor. I'm not in any danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as other men your age and height?\n\nPatient: Same as other guys? I don't know, I haven't really... I mean, I don't go around comparing myself to the other guys on the dock. Most of them are carrying a lot of extra baggage, if you ask me. \n\nTake Big Mike, for instance. We call him Big Mike because, well, the guy's built like a forklift. He must eat three meatball subs a day from that deli down on 4th Street. You know the one? They wrap the subs in that thick butcher paper, but the grease still soaks right through. Mike leaves those greasy wrappers all over the breakroom tables. It drives the janitor, Luis, absolutely crazy. Luis is this quiet guy, always listening to Spanish talk radio on his little portable speaker while he mops. He uses this industrial floor cleaner that smells like industrial pine and bleach. It's strong stuff. Actually, last winter, the heating system in the warehouse went out, and Luis accidentally left a bucket of that cleaner right by the loading bay doors. The whole bucket froze solid. We had to use a crowbar to chip it out so we could move the pallets. Took us an hour just to clear the ice. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really pay attention to what I weigh compared to guys like Mike or anyone else. I'm just focused on my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight if you eat a normal amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... afraid? It's just... if I eat a normal amount, like if I have rice or bread, or heaven forbid something greasy, I just... I can't stop thinking about it. I feel like it's just going to sit on me right away. He had a whole table of carburetors and spark plugs, just covered in this fine layer of rust and dust. His dog, this massive Rottweiler mix, was just sleeping under the folding table the whole time. It was so hot that day, I think it was August of '09, the asphalt was literally soft under my boots. I mean, change my appearance... yeah, I guess so. It's more about what it means, you know? When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like... if I'm not smaller and tight-looking, then I'm just not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. It's about standards. 

Speaking of standards, they really lowered them when they bought those new forklifts last year. The old ones, the yellow Komatsus, those things were built like tanks. You could run them for a twelve-hour shift and they wouldn't even hiccup. The new ones are electric, which I guess is supposed to be better for the environment or whatever, but they're so quiet. It's actually a safety hazard if you ask me. Last month, Dave—he's the guy who usually works the loading dock on Tuesdays—he almost backed one right into a stack of wooden pallets because he couldn't hear the motor over the sound of the rain hitting the tin roof. We get these massive thunderstorms in the spring, the kind that just rattle the whole building. The rain comes down so hard it sounds like a thousand hammers hitting the metal. I actually love that sound. Reminds me of when I was a kid and we had this old aluminum camper trailer... 

But yeah, the mirror thing. If my waist isn't tight, it just feels like I'm losing my edge, like I'm getting soft and undisciplined. I can't let that happen.

My sister was always trying to knit me these thick wool socks back then, but wool just makes my feet sweat in the work boots, and once your boots are wet, you're miserable all shift. \n\nBut yeah, they're just blowing the whole weight thing out of proportion. I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I'm terrified of it, but... I mean, yeah, I guess I do worry about it. If I eat rice, bread, or something greasy, I can’t stop thinking it will just sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or my belt the next morning to make sure I didn’t let myself go. You know how it is, you let your guard down for one second and suddenly everything's out of control. \n\nKind of like when they switched our inventory system at the warehouse back in '15. We used to do everything on paper, clipboards and carbon copies. It was a good system, you always had a hard copy if the computers went down. But then corporate brought in these new scanners, said it would make us \"more efficient.\" Half the guys didn't even know how to turn the things on. I remember Jimmy—he was a good guy, retired a few years back, moved down to Florida. He hated the humidity down there, said it ruined his golf swing. But anyway, Jimmy accidentally scanned the same pallet of engine blocks like five times, and the system thought we had enough inventory to supply a whole assembly line. The trucking company sent three extra rigs the next day just to pick up blocks we didn't even have. The logistics manager was screaming so loud his face turned purple. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I check my belt, make sure I'm still tight, still ready to move. You can't afford to get sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, I keep things pretty tight. Most days I just stick to black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little bit of tuna. If I'm not actually on the floor moving freight, I just tell myself I don't really need a real meal anyway. You don't need the fuel if the engine ain't running, right? \n\nSpeaking of engines, my buddy Carl—he works over at the auto parts distribution center across town—he was telling me about this whole shipment of alternators they got in last month. Apparently, they were all defective straight from the manufacturer. Just totally shot. Carl said they had to load 'em all back onto the pallets, wrap 'em up, and ship 'em right back to Mexico. The shrink wrap they use over there is garbage, though. It's that thin, cheap stuff that tears if you even look at it funny. I always tell our purchasing guy to buy the heavy-duty wrap, the 80-gauge stuff. It costs a few cents more a roll, but it saves you hours of re-wrapping when a pallet shifts in transit. I remember one time we had a whole skid of windshield washer fluid tip over inside a trailer because some rookie used the cheap wrap. Blue liquid everywhere. Smelled like windex for a month. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Just keep it light so I don't get weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a little weight?\n\nPatient: Look good? No, no way. If I gained weight... I mean, when I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, then I’m just... I'm not the disciplined man I used to be on the warehouse floor. You lose that edge, you know? \n\nIt’s like those old conveyor belts we had in the sorting facility before the remodel. They were these heavy, thick rubber belts, and over time, if they got even a little slack in them, they’d start catching on the rollers. Once a belt catches, it throws the whole line off. I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. We had guys standing around doing nothing for 48 hours while the freight just piled up on the dock. The regional manager came down, and he was livid, just screaming about metrics and throughput. But you can't argue with a broken motor, right? \n\nBut yeah, if I get soft around the middle, I just... I don't look right. I have to stay tight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I mean, my sister keeps harping on it, and my regular doctor acts like I'm about to drop dead or something. They keep saying I'm in some kind of danger, but they're making a mountain out of a molehill. They just don't get it. I'm just keeping myself light, you know? Sharp. Ready for work. If you're hauling freight, you can't be dragging extra weight around. \n\nSpeaking of hauling freight, we had this new guy start last week, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Showed up on his first day wearing these pristine, bright white sneakers. I took one look at him and told him those shoes wouldn't last till lunch. Sure enough, by ten o'clock he dropped a pallet jack right on his toe. Didn't break it, but the grease stain on that white mesh... ruined 'em. I always tell these kids, you gotta get steel-toe boots. I bought my Red Wings back in 2018, and they're still holding up. I get them resoled every couple of years by this cobbler downtown. Real old-school guy, his shop smells like leather polish and cigar smoke. He uses this heavy-duty stitching that you just don't see anymore. Most boots you buy off the shelf now, the soles start peeling off after six months because they just glue them on. It's cheap manufacturing, is what it is. \n\nBut yeah, my sister and the doctor... they just don't understand that I'm just staying sharp for the floor. I'm not in any danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as other men your age and height?\n\nPatient: Same as other guys? I don't know, I haven't really... I mean, I don't go around comparing myself to the other guys on the dock. Most of them are carrying a lot of extra baggage, if you ask me. \n\nTake Big Mike, for instance. We call him Big Mike because, well, the guy's built like a forklift. He must eat three meatball subs a day from that deli down on 4th Street. You know the one? They wrap the subs in that thick butcher paper, but the grease still soaks right through. Mike leaves those greasy wrappers all over the breakroom tables. It drives the janitor, Luis, absolutely crazy. Luis is this quiet guy, always listening to Spanish talk radio on his little portable speaker while he mops. He uses this industrial floor cleaner that smells like industrial pine and bleach. It's strong stuff. Actually, last winter, the heating system in the warehouse went out, and Luis accidentally left a bucket of that cleaner right by the loading bay doors. The whole bucket froze solid. We had to use a crowbar to chip it out so we could move the pallets. Took us an hour just to clear the ice. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really pay attention to what I weigh compared to guys like Mike or anyone else. I'm just focused on my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight if you eat a normal amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... afraid? It's just... if I eat a normal amount, like if I have rice or bread, or heaven forbid something greasy, I just... I can't stop thinking about it. I feel like it's just going to sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or pulling at my belt the next morning just to make sure I didn't let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of belts, I've had the same leather work belt for fifteen years. It's got this heavy brass buckle that I bought at a flea market out by the old fairgrounds. You remember when they used to host that massive swap meet every summer? People would come from three states over just to sell junk out of their trunks. I bought that buckle from this guy selling vintage motorcycle parts. He had a whole table of carburetors and spark plugs, just covered in this fine layer of rust and dust. His dog, this massive Rottweiler mix, was just sleeping under the folding table the whole time. It was so hot that day, I think it was August of '09, the asphalt was literally soft under my boots. You could smell the funnel cakes from the food trucks mixing with the smell of exhaust... \n\nBut yeah, if I eat that stuff, I just have to check the belt the next day to make sure I'm still tight. I can't afford to get soft.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it would change your appearance in a way that you couldn't accept?\n\nPatient: I mean, change my appearance... yeah, I guess so. It's more about what it means, you know? When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like... if I'm not smaller and tight-looking, then I'm just not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. It's about standards. \n\nSpeaking of standards, they really lowered them when they bought those new forklifts last year. The old ones, the yellow Komatsus, those things were built like tanks. You could run them for a twelve-hour shift and they wouldn't even hiccup. The new ones are electric, which I guess is supposed to be better for the environment or whatever, but they're so quiet. It's actually a safety hazard if you ask me. Last month, Dave—he's the guy who usually works the loading dock on Tuesdays—he almost backed one right into a stack of wooden pallets because he couldn't hear the motor over the sound of the rain hitting the tin roof. We get these massive thunderstorms in the spring, the kind that just rattle the whole building. The rain comes down so hard it sounds like a thousand hammers hitting the metal. I actually love that sound. Reminds me of when I was a kid and we had this old aluminum camper trailer... \n\nBut yeah, the mirror thing. If my waist isn't tight, it just feels like I'm losing my edge, like I'm getting soft and undisciplined. I can't let that happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Look the way I actually am? I don't really know... I mean, I'm not sure what you mean by that. A mirror's just a piece of glass, right? It just shows what's standing in front of it. 

Speaking of mirrors, though, we have these massive convex safety mirrors bolted up at all the blind intersections in the warehouse. You know the ones? The big round domes so you can see if a forklift is coming around the pallet racks. They get absolutely caked in cardboard dust. Cardboard dust is the worst thing in the world, I swear. It gets into everything—your hair, your boots, your lungs. I've been blowing my nose and seeing brown for twenty years. Anyway, management finally hired this outside crew last month to come in and wipe down all the high beams and those safety mirrors. They brought in this huge electric scissor lift that beeped every single time it moved an inch. Just this loud, piercing beep echoing off the concrete floors all shift long. It drove our dispatch guy, Gary, totally out of his mind. Gary’s already a high-strung guy, always chewing on those little plastic coffee stirrers until they're flat. He was practically screaming over the two-way radio trying to get the crew to turn the backup alarm off, but they didn't speak much English, so it was just a whole mess...

But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, looking in the mirror. Yeah, I don't know. I just look at it and see me, I guess. I haven't really thought about it being any different from reality.

The draft that came through that hole all winter was brutal. We had to wear double thermals just to feel our fingers. I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. I have to stay tight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I mean, my sister keeps harping on it, and my regular doctor acts like I'm about to drop dead or something. They keep saying I'm in some kind of danger, but they're making a mountain out of a molehill. They just don't get it. I'm just keeping myself light, you know? Sharp. Ready for work. If you're hauling freight, you can't be dragging extra weight around. \n\nSpeaking of hauling freight, we had this new guy start last week, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Showed up on his first day wearing these pristine, bright white sneakers. I took one look at him and told him those shoes wouldn't last till lunch. Sure enough, by ten o'clock he dropped a pallet jack right on his toe. Didn't break it, but the grease stain on that white mesh... ruined 'em. I always tell these kids, you gotta get steel-toe boots. I bought my Red Wings back in 2018, and they're still holding up. I get them resoled every couple of years by this cobbler downtown. Real old-school guy, his shop smells like leather polish and cigar smoke. He uses this heavy-duty stitching that you just don't see anymore. Most boots you buy off the shelf now, the soles start peeling off after six months because they just glue them on. It's cheap manufacturing, is what it is. \n\nBut yeah, my sister and the doctor... they just don't understand that I'm just staying sharp for the floor. I'm not in any danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as other men your age and height?\n\nPatient: Same as other guys? I don't know, I haven't really... I mean, I don't go around comparing myself to the other guys on the dock. Most of them are carrying a lot of extra baggage, if you ask me. \n\nTake Big Mike, for instance. We call him Big Mike because, well, the guy's built like a forklift. He must eat three meatball subs a day from that deli down on 4th Street. You know the one? They wrap the subs in that thick butcher paper, but the grease still soaks right through. Mike leaves those greasy wrappers all over the breakroom tables. It drives the janitor, Luis, absolutely crazy. Luis is this quiet guy, always listening to Spanish talk radio on his little portable speaker while he mops. He uses this industrial floor cleaner that smells like industrial pine and bleach. It's strong stuff. Actually, last winter, the heating system in the warehouse went out, and Luis accidentally left a bucket of that cleaner right by the loading bay doors. The whole bucket froze solid. We had to use a crowbar to chip it out so we could move the pallets. Took us an hour just to clear the ice. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really pay attention to what I weigh compared to guys like Mike or anyone else. I'm just focused on my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight if you eat a normal amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... afraid? It's just... if I eat a normal amount, like if I have rice or bread, or heaven forbid something greasy, I just... I can't stop thinking about it. I feel like it's just going to sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or pulling at my belt the next morning just to make sure I didn't let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of belts, I've had the same leather work belt for fifteen years. It's got this heavy brass buckle that I bought at a flea market out by the old fairgrounds. You remember when they used to host that massive swap meet every summer? People would come from three states over just to sell junk out of their trunks. I bought that buckle from this guy selling vintage motorcycle parts. He had a whole table of carburetors and spark plugs, just covered in this fine layer of rust and dust. His dog, this massive Rottweiler mix, was just sleeping under the folding table the whole time. It was so hot that day, I think it was August of '09, the asphalt was literally soft under my boots. You could smell the funnel cakes from the food trucks mixing with the smell of exhaust... \n\nBut yeah, if I eat that stuff, I just have to check the belt the next day to make sure I'm still tight. I can't afford to get soft.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it would change your appearance in a way that you couldn't accept?\n\nPatient: I mean, change my appearance... yeah, I guess so. It's more about what it means, you know? When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like... if I'm not smaller and tight-looking, then I'm just not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. It's about standards. \n\nSpeaking of standards, they really lowered them when they bought those new forklifts last year. The old ones, the yellow Komatsus, those things were built like tanks. You could run them for a twelve-hour shift and they wouldn't even hiccup. The new ones are electric, which I guess is supposed to be better for the environment or whatever, but they're so quiet. It's actually a safety hazard if you ask me. Last month, Dave—he's the guy who usually works the loading dock on Tuesdays—he almost backed one right into a stack of wooden pallets because he couldn't hear the motor over the sound of the rain hitting the tin roof. We get these massive thunderstorms in the spring, the kind that just rattle the whole building. The rain comes down so hard it sounds like a thousand hammers hitting the metal. I actually love that sound. Reminds me of when I was a kid and we had this old aluminum camper trailer... \n\nBut yeah, the mirror thing. If my waist isn't tight, it just feels like I'm losing my edge, like I'm getting soft and undisciplined. I can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you actually are when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Look the way I actually am? I don't really know... I mean, I'm not sure what you mean by that. A mirror's just a piece of glass, right? It just shows what's standing in front of it. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, though, we have these massive convex safety mirrors bolted up at all the blind intersections in the warehouse. You know the ones? The big round domes so you can see if a forklift is coming around the pallet racks. They get absolutely caked in cardboard dust. Cardboard dust is the worst thing in the world, I swear. It gets into everything—your hair, your boots, your lungs. I've been blowing my nose and seeing brown for twenty years. Anyway, management finally hired this outside crew last month to come in and wipe down all the high beams and those safety mirrors. They brought in this huge electric scissor lift that beeped every single time it moved an inch. Just this loud, piercing beep echoing off the concrete floors all shift long. It drove our dispatch guy, Gary, totally out of his mind. Gary’s already a high-strung guy, always chewing on those little plastic coffee stirrers until they're flat. He was practically screaming over the two-way radio trying to get the crew to turn the backup alarm off, but they didn't speak much English, so it was just a whole mess...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, looking in the mirror. Yeah, I don't know. I just look at it and see me, I guess. I haven't really thought about it being any different from reality.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Accurately represents how I look to other people? I... I don't really know how other people see me. I mean, they probably just see a guy in work boots, right? I don't really pay attention to what they're seeing. 

Speaking of paying attention, you really have to keep your eyes peeled when you're walking across the yard at work. The delivery trucks come flying in off the access road like they're qualifying for the Daytona 500. Just last week, this guy in a flatbed hauling corrugated steel—he must have been doing forty in a fifteen zone. He locked up his brakes right by the loading dock and left these massive black skid marks on the concrete. The smell of burnt rubber just hung in the air for hours. It reminded me of this time my brother and I went to a drag strip down in Texas back in the nineties. We sat in the bleachers all afternoon drinking cheap beer out of those flimsy plastic cups that sweat all over your hands. By the end of the day, our ears were ringing so bad we had to yell at each other in the car ride home just to have a conversation. My brother had this old Ford Ranger, a manual transmission, and the clutch was slipping so bad we barely made it up the hills...

But yeah, the mirror and other people. I don't really know. I just see what I see, and they see what they see. I don't think about it too much.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because the clinic for my logistics job wouldn't clear me. They said my weight was too low, which is just ridiculous if you ask me. I mean, to me, I just look lean, you know? Like someone who's ready to work, stay quick on his feet. You have to be quick in freight, otherwise you're just in the way. I remember this one guy, back in '98, big guy, thought he could muscle everything. But he was so slow, tripped over a pallet of dog food and nearly took out the whole racking system. It took us three hours to clean up that mess, and the smell of that dog food... man, it just lingered for days. I can still smell it sometimes when the humidity gets high. But yeah, they said I'm too light to work, which makes no sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, my sister and that clinic doctor keep saying I’m in some kind of danger. But they're just making it way bigger than it is. They don’t understand that I’m just keeping myself light, sharp, and ready for work. You gotta be sharp on the floor. \n\nJust last week, this new kid—barely twenty, I swear—was driving the forklift like an absolute maniac. Came whipping right around the blind corner near the loading docks. If I wasn't light on my feet, he would've pinned me right against the corrugated steel wall. And let me tell you, those steel panels aren't cheap to replace. We had a driver back in 2012 back a whole rig into one of the bay doors, and it took management three months to get the contractor out to fix it. The draft that came through that hole all winter was brutal. We had to wear double thermals just to feel our fingers. My sister was always trying to knit me these thick wool socks back then, but wool just makes my feet sweat in the work boots, and once your boots are wet, you're miserable all shift. \n\nBut yeah, they're just blowing the whole weight thing out of proportion. I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I'm terrified of it, but... I mean, yeah, I guess I do worry about it. If I eat rice, bread, or something greasy, I can’t stop thinking it will just sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or my belt the next morning to make sure I didn’t let myself go. You know how it is, you let your guard down for one second and suddenly everything's out of control. \n\nKind of like when they switched our inventory system at the warehouse back in '15. We used to do everything on paper, clipboards and carbon copies. It was a good system, you always had a hard copy if the computers went down. But then corporate brought in these new scanners, said it would make us \"more efficient.\" Half the guys didn't even know how to turn the things on. I remember Jimmy—he was a good guy, retired a few years back, moved down to Florida. He hated the humidity down there, said it ruined his golf swing. But anyway, Jimmy accidentally scanned the same pallet of engine blocks like five times, and the system thought we had enough inventory to supply a whole assembly line. The trucking company sent three extra rigs the next day just to pick up blocks we didn't even have. The logistics manager was screaming so loud his face turned purple. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I check my belt, make sure I'm still tight, still ready to move. You can't afford to get sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, I keep things pretty tight. Most days I just stick to black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little bit of tuna. If I'm not actually on the floor moving freight, I just tell myself I don't really need a real meal anyway. You don't need the fuel if the engine ain't running, right? \n\nSpeaking of engines, my buddy Carl—he works over at the auto parts distribution center across town—he was telling me about this whole shipment of alternators they got in last month. Apparently, they were all defective straight from the manufacturer. Just totally shot. Carl said they had to load 'em all back onto the pallets, wrap 'em up, and ship 'em right back to Mexico. The shrink wrap they use over there is garbage, though. It's that thin, cheap stuff that tears if you even look at it funny. I always tell our purchasing guy to buy the heavy-duty wrap, the 80-gauge stuff. It costs a few cents more a roll, but it saves you hours of re-wrapping when a pallet shifts in transit. I remember one time we had a whole skid of windshield washer fluid tip over inside a trailer because some rookie used the cheap wrap. Blue liquid everywhere. Smelled like windex for a month. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Just keep it light so I don't get weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a little weight?\n\nPatient: Look good? No, no way. If I gained weight... I mean, when I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, then I’m just... I'm not the disciplined man I used to be on the warehouse floor. You lose that edge, you know? \n\nIt’s like those old conveyor belts we had in the sorting facility before the remodel. They were these heavy, thick rubber belts, and over time, if they got even a little slack in them, they’d start catching on the rollers. Once a belt catches, it throws the whole line off. I remember one Tuesday, right before the holiday rush, the main line jammed up so bad it sheared the drive pin right off the motor. The maintenance guy, old man Henderson—he always smelled like peppermint and WD-40—he had to order a custom pin from this shop three towns over. Took them two days to machine the thing. We had guys standing around doing nothing for 48 hours while the freight just piled up on the dock. The regional manager came down, and he was livid, just screaming about metrics and throughput. But you can't argue with a broken motor, right? \n\nBut yeah, if I get soft around the middle, I just... I don't look right. I have to stay tight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I mean, my sister keeps harping on it, and my regular doctor acts like I'm about to drop dead or something. They keep saying I'm in some kind of danger, but they're making a mountain out of a molehill. They just don't get it. I'm just keeping myself light, you know? Sharp. Ready for work. If you're hauling freight, you can't be dragging extra weight around. \n\nSpeaking of hauling freight, we had this new guy start last week, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Showed up on his first day wearing these pristine, bright white sneakers. I took one look at him and told him those shoes wouldn't last till lunch. Sure enough, by ten o'clock he dropped a pallet jack right on his toe. Didn't break it, but the grease stain on that white mesh... ruined 'em. I always tell these kids, you gotta get steel-toe boots. I bought my Red Wings back in 2018, and they're still holding up. I get them resoled every couple of years by this cobbler downtown. Real old-school guy, his shop smells like leather polish and cigar smoke. He uses this heavy-duty stitching that you just don't see anymore. Most boots you buy off the shelf now, the soles start peeling off after six months because they just glue them on. It's cheap manufacturing, is what it is. \n\nBut yeah, my sister and the doctor... they just don't understand that I'm just staying sharp for the floor. I'm not in any danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as other men your age and height?\n\nPatient: Same as other guys? I don't know, I haven't really... I mean, I don't go around comparing myself to the other guys on the dock. Most of them are carrying a lot of extra baggage, if you ask me. \n\nTake Big Mike, for instance. We call him Big Mike because, well, the guy's built like a forklift. He must eat three meatball subs a day from that deli down on 4th Street. You know the one? They wrap the subs in that thick butcher paper, but the grease still soaks right through. Mike leaves those greasy wrappers all over the breakroom tables. It drives the janitor, Luis, absolutely crazy. Luis is this quiet guy, always listening to Spanish talk radio on his little portable speaker while he mops. He uses this industrial floor cleaner that smells like industrial pine and bleach. It's strong stuff. Actually, last winter, the heating system in the warehouse went out, and Luis accidentally left a bucket of that cleaner right by the loading bay doors. The whole bucket froze solid. We had to use a crowbar to chip it out so we could move the pallets. Took us an hour just to clear the ice. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really pay attention to what I weigh compared to guys like Mike or anyone else. I'm just focused on my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight if you eat a normal amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... afraid? It's just... if I eat a normal amount, like if I have rice or bread, or heaven forbid something greasy, I just... I can't stop thinking about it. I feel like it's just going to sit on me right away. I'll be checking the scale or pulling at my belt the next morning just to make sure I didn't let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of belts, I've had the same leather work belt for fifteen years. It's got this heavy brass buckle that I bought at a flea market out by the old fairgrounds. You remember when they used to host that massive swap meet every summer? People would come from three states over just to sell junk out of their trunks. I bought that buckle from this guy selling vintage motorcycle parts. He had a whole table of carburetors and spark plugs, just covered in this fine layer of rust and dust. His dog, this massive Rottweiler mix, was just sleeping under the folding table the whole time. It was so hot that day, I think it was August of '09, the asphalt was literally soft under my boots. You could smell the funnel cakes from the food trucks mixing with the smell of exhaust... \n\nBut yeah, if I eat that stuff, I just have to check the belt the next day to make sure I'm still tight. I can't afford to get soft.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it would change your appearance in a way that you couldn't accept?\n\nPatient: I mean, change my appearance... yeah, I guess so. It's more about what it means, you know? When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, I feel like... if I'm not smaller and tight-looking, then I'm just not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. It's about standards. \n\nSpeaking of standards, they really lowered them when they bought those new forklifts last year. The old ones, the yellow Komatsus, those things were built like tanks. You could run them for a twelve-hour shift and they wouldn't even hiccup. The new ones are electric, which I guess is supposed to be better for the environment or whatever, but they're so quiet. It's actually a safety hazard if you ask me. Last month, Dave—he's the guy who usually works the loading dock on Tuesdays—he almost backed one right into a stack of wooden pallets because he couldn't hear the motor over the sound of the rain hitting the tin roof. We get these massive thunderstorms in the spring, the kind that just rattle the whole building. The rain comes down so hard it sounds like a thousand hammers hitting the metal. I actually love that sound. Reminds me of when I was a kid and we had this old aluminum camper trailer... \n\nBut yeah, the mirror thing. If my waist isn't tight, it just feels like I'm losing my edge, like I'm getting soft and undisciplined. I can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you actually are when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Look the way I actually am? I don't really know... I mean, I'm not sure what you mean by that. A mirror's just a piece of glass, right? It just shows what's standing in front of it. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, though, we have these massive convex safety mirrors bolted up at all the blind intersections in the warehouse. You know the ones? The big round domes so you can see if a forklift is coming around the pallet racks. They get absolutely caked in cardboard dust. Cardboard dust is the worst thing in the world, I swear. It gets into everything—your hair, your boots, your lungs. I've been blowing my nose and seeing brown for twenty years. Anyway, management finally hired this outside crew last month to come in and wipe down all the high beams and those safety mirrors. They brought in this huge electric scissor lift that beeped every single time it moved an inch. Just this loud, piercing beep echoing off the concrete floors all shift long. It drove our dispatch guy, Gary, totally out of his mind. Gary’s already a high-strung guy, always chewing on those little plastic coffee stirrers until they're flat. He was practically screaming over the two-way radio trying to get the crew to turn the backup alarm off, but they didn't speak much English, so it was just a whole mess...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, looking in the mirror. Yeah, I don't know. I just look at it and see me, I guess. I haven't really thought about it being any different from reality.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mirror reflection accurately represents how you look to other people?\n\nPatient: Accurately represents how I look to other people? I... I don't really know how other people see me. I mean, they probably just see a guy in work boots, right? I don't really pay attention to what they're seeing. \n\nSpeaking of paying attention, you really have to keep your eyes peeled when you're walking across the yard at work. The delivery trucks come flying in off the access road like they're qualifying for the Daytona 500. Just last week, this guy in a flatbed hauling corrugated steel—he must have been doing forty in a fifteen zone. He locked up his brakes right by the loading dock and left these massive black skid marks on the concrete. The smell of burnt rubber just hung in the air for hours. It reminded me of this time my brother and I went to a drag strip down in Texas back in the nineties. We sat in the bleachers all afternoon drinking cheap beer out of those flimsy plastic cups that sweat all over your hands. By the end of the day, our ears were ringing so bad we had to yell at each other in the car ride home just to have a conversation. My brother had this old Ford Ranger, a manual transmission, and the clutch was slipping so bad we barely made it up the hills...\n\nBut yeah, the mirror and other people. I don't really know. I just see what I see, and they see what they see. I don't think about it too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you actually are when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Look the way I actually am? I don't really know... I mean, I'm not sure what you mean by that. A mirror's just a piece of glass, right? It just shows what's standing in front of it. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, though, we have these massive convex safety mirrors bolted up at all the blind intersections in the warehouse. You know the ones? The big round domes so you can see if a forklift is coming around the pallet racks. They get absolutely caked in cardboard dust. Cardboard dust is the worst thing in the world, I swear. It gets into everything—your hair, your boots, your lungs. I've been blowing my nose and seeing brown for twenty years. Anyway, management finally hired this outside crew last month to come in and wipe down all the high beams and those safety mirrors. They brought in this huge electric scissor lift that beeped every single time it moved an inch. Just this loud, piercing beep echoing off the concrete floors all shift long. It drove our dispatch guy, Gary, totally out of his mind. Gary’s already a high-strung guy, always chewing on those little plastic coffee stirrers until they're flat. He was practically screaming over the two-way radio trying to get the crew to turn the backup alarm off, but they didn't speak much English, so it was just a whole mess...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, looking in the mirror. Yeah, I don't know. I just look at it and see me, I guess. I haven't really thought about it being any different from reality.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mirror reflection accurately represents how you look to other people?\n\nPatient: Accurately represents how I look to other people? I... I don't really know how other people see me. I mean, they probably just see a guy in work boots, right? I don't really pay attention to what they're seeing. \n\nSpeaking of paying attention, you really have to keep your eyes peeled when you're walking across the yard at work. The delivery trucks come flying in off the access road like they're qualifying for the Daytona 500. Just last week, this guy in a flatbed hauling corrugated steel—he must have been doing forty in a fifteen zone. He locked up his brakes right by the loading dock and left these massive black skid marks on the concrete. The smell of burnt rubber just hung in the air for hours. It reminded me of this time my brother and I went to a drag strip down in Texas back in the nineties. We sat in the bleachers all afternoon drinking cheap beer out of those flimsy plastic cups that sweat all over your hands. By the end of the day, our ears were ringing so bad we had to yell at each other in the car ride home just to have a conversation. My brother had this old Ford Ranger, a manual transmission, and the clutch was slipping so bad we barely made it up the hills...\n\nBut yeah, the mirror and other people. I don't really know. I just see what I see, and they see what they see. I don't think about it too much.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how others see it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Difference between how I see it and how others see it? Man, I couldn't tell you. People don't exactly come up to me on the warehouse floor and critique my waistline, you know? I'm not really sure what they see. 

Though, speaking of how things look, they just changed out all the overhead lights in the main sorting area last weekend. Swapped out the old buzzing fluorescents for these high-efficiency LED panels. Let me tell you, it completely messes with your eyes. Everything looks almost... blue now? It's like working in a hospital or a spaceship or something. My buddy Mike—he runs the shrink-wrap machine—he's been complaining all week that the new lights are giving him a migraine. He even brought in these tinted safety glasses, the kind guys wear when they're doing light welding, just to take the edge off. Management told him it was a safety violation because the lenses were too dark, and they got into this huge shouting match right there by the time clock. Mike’s been there thirty years, he’s not gonna take crap from some twenty-something shift supervisor who just got his degree. It was a whole scene, everyone just stopped loading the conveyor belt to watch... 

Anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, how others see my body. Yeah, I have no clue. I just keep to myself mostly, so I don't really know if they see something different than I do.
